u1426 [T1 R1 C2] AWH.C.5.4 Describe the Hebrew Bible’s account of the unification of the tribes of Israel under Kings Saul, David, and Solomon, including David’s founding of Jerusalem in 1000 BC and the building of the first temple by Solomon.
u1427 [T1 R2 C2] AWH.C.5.5 Examine the Assyrian and Babylonian conquests of Israel and Judah, the Babylonian Exile, the return to Israel, and the establishment of the Maccabean and Herodian dynasties.
u1428 [T1 R3 C2] AWH.C.5.6 Explain the expulsion/dispersion of the Jews to other lands (Diaspora) after the destruction of the second temple in Jerusalem in AD 70 and the renaming of the region by the Romans.
u1429 [T1 R4 C2] AWH.C.5.7 Explain how Judaism influenced the foundation of Christianity, and how these religions contributed to the foundations of Western Civilization.
u1430 [T1 R5 C1] AWH.C.6 The student will analyze the roots of Western Civilization in Ancient Greece.
u1431 [T1 R5 C2] AWH.C.6.1 Locate on a historical map of the Mediterranean area Ancient Greece, tracing the extent of its influence to 300 BC, including the locations and historical significance of architectural sites, such as Knossos, Periclean Athens, the Parthenon, and the Acropolis.
u1432 [T1 R6 C2] AWH.C.6.2 Explain how the geographical location of Athens and other city-states contributed to their role in maritime trade, colonies in the Mediterranean, and the expansion of Greek culture.
u1433 [T1 R7 C2] AWH.C.6.3 Analyze why the government of ancient Athens is considered the beginning of democratic government by explaining the political concepts developed in ancient Greece (e.g., “polis” or city-state, rule of law, liberty, civic participation and voting rights, legislative bodies, and Constitutions) and analyzing the effects of The Athenian Constitution.
u1434 [T1 R8 C2] AWH.C.6.4 Describe the lasting impact of major accomplishments of the ancient Greeks. A. Greek institutions: the lyceum, the gymnasium, the Library of Alexandria B. Mathematics: Pythagoras and Euclid C. Medicine: Hippocrates D. Science and Engineering: Archimedes, Ptolemy E. History, Poetry, Drama: Herodotus, Homer, Aeschylus F. Language: development of a complete alphabet, ancestor of the Latin alphabet.
u1435 [T1 R9 C2] AWH.C.6.5 Analyze the causes, course, and consequences of the Persian and Peloponnesian Wars, including the significance of Pericles’ Funeral Oration.
=== page 151 === [running header: Oklahoma Academic Standards for Social Studies]
u1438 [T1 R1 C2] AWH.C.6.6 Describe the rise of Alexander the Great and the spread of Greek culture, evaluating major contributions of Hellenistic art, philosophy, science, and political thought.
u1439 [T1 R2 C1] AWH.C.7 The student will analyze the roots of Western Civilization in the Roman Republic and Empire.
u1440 [T1 R2 C2] AWH.C.7.1 Locate on a historical map ancient Rome, tracing the changing boundaries of the Roman Republic and Empire from 500 BC to 500 AD and explaining how the geographical location of ancient Rome contributed to the expansion of its political power in the Mediterranean region and beyond.
u1441 [T1 R3 C2] AWH.C.7.2 Explain the rise of the Roman Republic and the role of historical figures in Roman history, including Brutus, Cincinnatus, and Hannibal.
u1442 [T1 R4 C2] AWH.C.7.3 Examine the government of the Roman Republic and its contribution to the development of republican self-government (e.g., the concepts of separation of powers, liberty, the rule of law, representative government, civic duty).
u1443 [T1 R5 C2] AWH.C.7.4 Analyze the influence of Greek civilization on Roman art, literature, society, and government.
u1444 [T1 R6 C2] AWH.C.7.5 Describe the collapse of the Roman Republic from the agrarian reform law of Tiberius Gracchus to the establishment of Julius Caesar’s dictatorship.
u1445 [T1 R7 C2] AWH.C.7.6 Examine Julius Caesar’s and Augustus’ transformation of Rome from a republic to an empire.
u1446 [T1 R8 C2] AWH.C.7.7 Explain the reasons for the growth and longevity of the Roman Empire (e.g., military organization, tactics, conquests, functions of taxes, use of a standard currency, road construction, protection of trade routes, benefits of a Pax Romana, network of Roman roads and aqueducts).
u1447 [T1 R9 C2] AWH.C.7.8 Describe the central features of Christianity in ancient Rome (e.g., monotheism, belief and teachings of Jesus of Nazareth as the messiah, the influence of the Hebrew Bible on the Christian Old and New Testaments, the influence of the apostle Paul, and the meanings and effects of Jesus of Nazareth’s words as recorded by Matthew).
=== page 152 === [running header: Oklahoma Academic Standards for Social Studies]
u1450 [T1 R1 C2] AWH.C.7.9 Examine the early expansion of Christianity, including the relationship of early Christians, Jews, and officials of the Roman Empire, the relationship of Christianity to Greek thought (logos), the effects of state persecution, the impact of the Council of Nicaea, and the significance of the Nicene Creed.
u1451 [T1 R2 C2] AWH.C.7.10 Explain how internal forces (e.g., the rise of autonomous military powers, political corruption, economic and political instability) and external forces (e.g., shrinking trade, attacks, and invasions) led to the disintegration of the Roman Empire.
u1452 [T1 R3 C2] AWH.C.7.11 Describe the Sack of Rome, fall of the Western Roman Empire, and the establishment of Germanic kingdoms (e.g., Ostrogoths, Visigoths, Vandals, Franks, Anglo-Saxon kingdoms).
u1453 [T1 R4 C2] AWH.C.7.12 Explain the contribution of Roman civilization to law, literature, architecture, engineering, and technology, including the spread and influence of the Latin language.
u1454 [T1 R5 C1] AWH.C.8 The student will examine the foundations of ancient Indian, Chinese, and African civilizations.
u1455 [T1 R5 C2] AWH.C.8.1 Describe the origins of Indian civilizations such as those of the Indus Valley, the evolution and central principles of Hinduism and the development of early Indian societies and their organizational structures.
u1456 [T1 R6 C2] AWH.C.8.2 Explain the origins and significant aspects of Buddhism, including the life and teachings of Siddhartha Gautama, the evolution and central principles of Buddhism, and the significance of early Indian Hindu and Buddhist monarchies.
u1457 [T1 R7 C2] AWH.C.8.3 Describe important political, economic, and cultural aspects of Indian history, including the Maurya and Kushan Empires, the Gupta Empire, Golden Age of India, Indian mathematics, and maritime expansion.
u1458 [T1 R8 C2] AWH.C.8.4 Explain the origins of Shintoism and its central feature of ritual practices carried out with discipline to maintain connections with the ancient past.
u1459 [T1 R9 C2] AWH.C.8.5 Examine significant aspects of Confucian philosophy and its role in the continuity of Chinese civilization, focusing on the life and teachings of Confucius, the significance of individual ethics, and maintenance of order, hierarchy, and political power.

u1462 [T1 R1 C2] AWH.C.8.6 Assess the significance and legacy of political, economic, and cultural aspects of the Qin, Han, and Tang dynasties (e.g., unification of China, construction of the Great Wall of China, conquest of southern China, introduction of Buddhism through cultural exchange with India, agriculture and transportation, redistribution systems, and inventions of the compass, gunpowder, papermaking, and printing).
u1463 [T1 R2 C2] AWH.C.8.7 Analyze the emergence of complex, interregional networks of trade throughout Afro-Eurasia and how trade networks led to the diffusion and evolution of ideas, resources, and technologies.
u1464 [T1 R3 C2] AWH.C.8.8 Explain the significance of the Aksum Kingdom to the region of North Africa, including its role as a center for trade between Europe and Asia, its development of written script, and its adoption of Christianity, establishing a legacy in the modern Ethiopian Orthodox Church.
u1465 [T1 R4 C1] AWH.C.9 The student will examine characteristics of the Early Middle Ages in Europe from 476 to 1000.
u1466 [T1 R4 C2] AWH.C.9.1 Describe the preservation of Greek and Roman traditions, the Code of Justinian, and the construction of the Church of the Holy Wisdom or Hagia Sophia.
u1467 [T1 R5 C2] AWH.C.9.2 Explain the growth and influence of Christianity and the Catholic Church in medieval society, including the significance of Europe’s network of medieval pilgrimage routes, the role of monasteries in preserving the culture of antiquity, and the spread of Christianity to Northern and Eastern Europe.
u1468 [T1 R6 C2] AWH.C.9.3 Examine the founding and central features of Islam (e.g., Muhammad’s visions, the Qur’an, pilgrimage to Mecca, and the concept of monotheism).
u1469 [T1 R7 C2] AWH.C.9.4 Describe the impact and legacy of Islam and the Islamic empire, including discoveries in science and mathematics, and the spread of Islam in the Middle East, Africa, and Europe.
u1470 [T1 R8 C2] AWH.C.9.5 Analyze the Latin West following the fall of the Roman Empire. A. Explain the structure of feudal society and its economic, political, and social effects, including the differing orders of medieval society. B. Describe the rise of Frankish kings, including the roles of Charles Martel, influence of Charlemagne, and the establishment of the Holy Roman Empire. C. Trace the rise of Anglo-Saxon England, the Danish invasions, and the influence of Alfred the Great. D. Describe Scandinavian raids and conquests (Vikings), explorations (e.g., Iceland, Greenland, Vinland), and state-building (e.g., Normandy, England, Naples, Russia).

u1473 [T1 R1 C1] AWH.C.10 The student will examine change during High Middle Ages in Europe from 1000-1300.
u1474 [T1 R1 C2] AWH.C.10.1 Explain the origins of iconoclasm and the schism between Orthodoxy and Catholicism in 1054.
u1475 [T1 R2 C2] AWH.C.10.2 Examine how William the Conqueror at the Battle of Hastings established the French- speaking Normans as rulers of England and brought about a significant cultural, economic and military transformation.
u1476 [T1 R3 C2] AWH.C.10.3 Describe political developments in medieval Europe from the Norman Conquest to the Mongol Invasions, including the A. development of English Common Law, including Henry II’s consolidation of royal power, reorganization of the judicial system, and establishment of criminal justice procedures B. partial disintegration of the Holy Roman Empire and the rise of independent city-states in Italy and Germany C. consequences of the European Crusades in the 11th to 13th centuries, including the Fourth Crusade, the Crusader conquest of Orthodox Christian Byzantium, and the Crusader Kingdoms of Greece D. divergence of Russia from Western Europe (e.g., Orthodox Christianity, Baltic Crusades, the Mongol Yoke).
u1477 [T1 R4 C2] AWH.C.10.4 Analyze the origins, meanings, immediate effects, and legacy of the Magna Carta.
u1478 [T1 R5 C2] AWH.C.10.5 Examine religious, intellectual, cultural, technological, and economic developments in medieval Europe from the Norman Conquest to 1300. A. Describe the clash between papacy and empire with regards to the development of law. B. Identify the growing influence of universities, Roman law, canon law, and Scholasticism. C. Describe significant aspects of medieval culture, including architecture. music, art, and literature. D. Explain the emergence of a modern economy, the growth of commerce, towns, and a merchant class. E. Identify technological and agricultural improvements (e.g., plough, windmills, watermills). F. Describe military improvements (e.g., mounted knight and fortifications). G. Evaluate the impact of Marco Polo’s travels to India and China.
u1479 [T1 R6 C1] AWH.C.11 The student will analyze the significant

u1483 [T1 R1 C1] advancements in Asian and African civilizations from 1000-1500.
u1484 [T1 R1 C2] AWH.C.11.2 Describe the rise and fall of the Mughal Empire, including important political, economic, and cultural aspects of early modern Indian history (e.g., rise and development of the Sikh religion and states, Portuguese maritime hegemony, Mughal rule and persecution of Hindus).
u1485 [T1 R2 C2] AWH.C.11.3 Examine the significant political, economic, and cultural aspects of the history of the Song, Yuan, and Ming dynasties (e.g.,the civil service, Mongol conquest, Construction of the Forbidden City, voyages of Zheng He, Ming political collapse, inventions such as movable-type printing, the cannon, and paper currency).
u1486 [T1 R3 C2] AWH.C.11.4 Examine the influence of the Mali Empire, with attention to the role Timbuktu played in control over West African trade and as a center of culture and learning during the Golden Age of Islam.
u1487 [T1 R4 C1] AWH.C.12 The student will analyze the significant changes and legacy of the Late Middle Ages from 1300 to 1450.
u1488 [T1 R4 C2] AWH.C.12.1 Describe internal divisions and impact on the Roman Catholic Church during the Avignon Papacy, the Great Schism, and the subsequent councils.
u1489 [T1 R5 C2] AWH.C.12.2 Explain developments in medieval English legal and constitutional history and their importance in the rise of modern institutions and self-government (e.g., common law, parliament, habeas corpus).
u1490 [T1 R6 C2] AWH.C.12.3 Assess the economic and social effects of the spread of the Black Death on society.
u1491 [T1 R7 C2] AWH.C.12.4 Describe the major events and lasting effects of the Hundred Years War, including the influence of Henry V and Joan of Arc.

u1496 [T1 R5 C2] History of 20th Century Totalitarianism
u1497 [T1 R8 C2] This course focuses on the analysis of totalitarian regimes of the 20th century, especially those based in Communist and Nazi ideology,
u1498 [T1 R9 C2] including nineteenth-century developments of Marxism and the precursors to Nazi’s antisemitic persecution of the Jewish people.
u1499 [T1 R10 C2] Students will also explore the Bolshevik Revolution, its assault on private property and establishment of a dictatorial one-party state.
u1500 [T1 R11 C2] From these studies, students will develop a deeper understanding of the importance of ethical behavior and personal dignity.

u1614 [T1 R8 C2] This course introduces students to the field of psychology, providing an opportunity to study the nature of psychological science.

u1621 [T1 R15 C4] PS.1.5 Compare quantitative and qualitative research strategies including experiments, surveys, focus groups, and narratives as the foundation of research in psychology.

u1652 [T1 R5 C2] Sociology Content Standards
u1653 [T1 R8 C2] Sociology is the study of social life, social change, and the social causes and consequences of human behavior. This course
u1654 [T1 R9 C2] investigates the structure of groups, organizations, and societies and analyzes how people interact within these contexts.
u1655 [T1 R11 C1] S.1 The student will explain the sociological perspective and identify sociology as a scientific field of inquiry.
u1656 [T1 R11 C4] S.1.1 Describe the development of the field of sociology as a social science, including the contributions of leading theorists within sociology.
u1657 [T1 R12 C4] S.1.2 Identify, differentiate among, and apply the major sociological theories (e.g., functionalist perspective, conflict theory, symbolic interactionism).
u1658 [T1 R13 C4] S.1.3 Evaluate the strengths and weaknesses of the major methods of sociological research, including surveys and interviews, experiments, observations, content analysis, and research ethics.
u1659 [T1 R14 C4] S.1.4 Apply the scientific method of inquiry on the study of society, including developing a hypothesis, and identifying independent and dependent variables.
u1660 [T1 R15 C1] S.2 The student will examine the influence of culture and the way cultural transmission is accomplished.
u1661 [T1 R15 C4] S.2.1 Describe culture and the components of culture, including norms and values, material and non-material culture, and explain the ways culture influences individuals.
u1662 [T1 R16 C4] S.2.2 Explain the process of the social construction of the self and analyze how culture influences individuals, groups, and subcultures.
u1663 [T1 R17 C1] S.3 Students will examine the social construction of groups and their impact on individuals.
u1664 [T1 R17 C4] S.3.1 Describe the process of socialization, examining how social groups are composed and why individuals become members of or associate with different social groups.
u1665 [T1 R18 C4] S.3.2 Describe various subcultures and countercultures and explain their influence according to sociological perspectives.
u1666 [T1 R19 C4] S.3.3 Analyze how culture influences individuals, including the mechanisms of ethnocentrism, cultural relativity, and culture shock.
u1667 [T1 R20 C4] S.3.4 Identify aspects of social structure, including social class, social status and roles, as well as their influences on individuals and groups.
=== page 170 === [running header: Oklahoma Academic Standards for Social Studies]
u1670 [T1 R1 C2] S.3.5 Describe status as a component of social structure, including status sets, ascribed and achieved statuses, status symbols and master status.
u1671 [T1 R2 C1] S.4 The student will analyze the effects of social institutions on group behavior over the life course of individuals.
u1672 [T1 R2 C2] S.4.1 Evaluate the characteristics of primary and secondary groups, including their influence over the behavior, attitudes, and values of their members.
u1673 [T1 R3 C2] S.4.2 Identify various reference groups and explain how they are used by individuals to evaluate themselves.
u1674 [T1 R4 C2] S.4.3 Examine the influence of major social institutions, including family, education, religion, and the media.
u1675 [T1 R5 C2] S.4.4 Examine the benefits of belonging to social groups, as well as the power of social groups over behavior and attitudes.
u1676 [T1 R6 C2] S.4.5 Explain how and why social institutions and cultures change over time.
u1677 [T1 R7 C1] S.5 The student will analyze social problems that affect large numbers of people within a social system.
u1678 [T1 R7 C2] S.5.1 Analyze patterns of social stratification and their effects on individuals and groups.
u1679 [T1 R8 C2] S.5.2 Describe social problems and distinguish between characteristics of a social problem versus an individual problem.
u1680 [T1 R9 C2] S.5.3 Analyze patterns of behavior found within social problems and their implications for society (e.g., juvenile crime, long-term unemployment).
u1681 [T1 R10 C2] S.5.4 Examine the extent to which individual and group responses influence potential resolutions to social problems.

u1684 Appendix: Social Studies Practices
u1685 [T1 R2 C1] I. Democratic Processes: Students will apply critical thinking skills to address authentic civic issues.
u1686 [T1 R5 C1] K-Grade 1
u1687 [T1 R5 C3] Grades 2-3
u1688 [T1 R5 C4] Grades 4-5
u1689 [T1 R5 C5] Grades 6-8
u1690 [T1 R5 C6] Grades 9-12
u1691 [T1 R8 C2] 1. The student will demonstrate an understanding of the virtue of civil discourse to analyze and address real-world problems.
u1692 [T1 R10 C1] A. (K-1) Describe how people can work together to make decisions in the classroom and school.
u1693 [T1 R10 C3] A. (2-3) Explain how people make decisions in a democracy, using examples from their community and state.
u1694 [T1 R10 C4] A. (4-5) Explain how human experiences can shape a person’s point of view about civic issues.
u1695 [T1 R10 C5] A. (6-8) Analyze why the acknowledgement of different perspectives can contribute to civil discourse and solutions to civic issues.
u1696 [T1 R10 C6] A. (9-12) Evaluate the impact of perspectives, civil discourse, and democratic principles on addressing civic issues.
u1697 [T1 R11 C1] B. (K-1) Engage in democratic processes to address authentic, real-world problems in the classroom or school.
u1698 [T1 R11 C3] B. (2-3) Use democratic processes to consider and propose actions to address authentic, real- world problems in the community and state.
u1699 [T1 R11 C4] B. (4-5) Use a range of democratic procedures to discuss and make decisions about real-world problems in the community, region, and nation.
u1700 [T1 R11 C5] B. (6-8) Apply a range of deliberative and democratic procedures to discuss, make decisions, and propose action about real-world problems in and out of school.
u1701 [T1 R11 C6] B. (9-12) Engage in a range of deliberative and democratic processes to develop strategies to address authentic, real-world problems in community and out-of-school contexts.
u1702 [T1 R12 C1] C. (K-1) Identify and discuss local problems and ways in which people are trying to address these problems.
u1703 [T1 R12 C3] C. (2-3) Describe a range of local and state problems and how communities and local governments are trying to address them.
u1704 [T1 R12 C4] C. (4-5) Explain challenges of the past and present and describe strategies used to address local, regional, or national problems.
u1705 [T1 R12 C5] C. (6-8) Use information to analyze how a specific problem can manifest itself in regional and global issues, describing options for solutions.
u1706 [T1 R12 C6] C. (9-12) Gather and evaluate information regarding complex problems, assessing individual and collective actions taken to address them.

u1709 [T1 R2 C2] 2. The student will develop practices which demonstrate an understanding that social studies involves the evaluation of evidence.
u1710 [T1 R4 C1] A. (K-1) Collaboratively ask and respond to essential questions of common concerns to the student and community.
u1711 [T1 R4 C3] A. (2-3) Ask and respond to enduring essential questions of common concerns to the community and state.
u1712 [T1 R4 C4] A. (4-5) Ask and examine essential questions that are important to others, as well as enduring across social studies topics.
u1713 [T1 R4 C5] A. (6-8) Investigate and propose answers to essential questions representing complex enduring issues across the social studies disciplines.
u1714 [T1 R4 C6] A. (9-12) Develop, investigate, and evaluate plausible answers to essential questions that reflect enduring understandings across time, real world circumstances, and social studies disciplines.
u1715 [T1 R5 C1] B. (K-1) Use supporting questions to help guide learning of specific social studies content.
u1716 [T1 R5 C3] B. (2-3) Develop responses to supporting questions relevant to specific social studies content knowledge.
u1717 [T1 R5 C4] B. (4-5) Compare points of agreement from reliable information used to answer supporting questions related to social studies content.
u1718 [T1 R5 C5] B. (6-8) Answer supporting questions related to social studies content knowledge and make connections to different interpretations.
u1719 [T1 R5 C6] B. (9-12) Evaluate points of agreement and disagreement from reliable information and expert interpretations used to answer supporting questions related to content knowledge.
u1720 [T1 R6 C1] C. (K-1) Practice critical thinking skills by responding to various levels of open-ended questions.
u1721 [T1 R6 C3] C. (2-3) Reinforce critical thinking skills by regularly asking and responding to levels of open-ended questions.
u1722 [T1 R6 C4] C. (4-5) Demonstrate critical thinking skills by frequently developing and answering various depth of knowledge questions.
u1723 [T1 R6 C5] C. (6-8) Develop deeper critical thinking skills by questioning assumptions and identifying inconsistencies or errors in reasoning.
u1724 [T1 R6 C6] C. (9-12) Reinforce critical thinking by evaluating and challenging ideas and assumptions; analyzing and explaining inconsistencies in reasoning.
u1725 [T1 R7 C1] D. (K-1) With guidance, demonstrate understanding of social studies content through completion of authentic tasks.
u1726 [T1 R7 C3] D. (2-3) Demonstrate understanding of social studies content through completion of authentic tasks and assessments.
u1727 [T1 R7 C4] D. (4-5) Reinforce understanding of social studies content through collaborative inquiries and the completion of authentic tasks and assessments.
u1728 [T1 R7 C5] C. (6-8) Demonstrate understanding of social studies content through the development of self-driven inquiries and the completion of authentic tasks and assessments.
u1729 [T1 R7 C6] D. (9-12) Demonstrate understanding of content through the development of self-driven inquiries and the completion of multi-staged, authentic tasks and assessments.

u1733 [T1 R3 C2] Students will use interdisciplinary tools to acquire, apply, and

u1739 [T1 R7 C4] Grades 4-5

u1742 [T1 R10 C2] 1. The student will understand the principles of government, the benefits of democratic systems, and their responsibilities as citizens.
u1743 [T1 R12 C1] A. (K-1) Identify democratic principles such as equality, fairness, and respect for the law.
u1744 [T1 R12 C3] A. (2-3) Describe civic virtues and democratic principles such as equality, fairness, and respect for the law.
u1745 [T1 R12 C4] A. (4-5) Explain democratic principles in historic documents by identifying examples of civic virtues and principles at work in state and national settings.
u1746 [T1 R12 C6] A. (6-8) Compare and analyze civic virtues and democratic principles in historic and global settings, explaining how they influence political institutions.
u1747 [T1 R12 C8] A. (9-12) Evaluate various significant documents from the United States and other nations to compare civic virtues and principles of political systems.
u1748 [T1 R13 C1] B. (K-1) Identify responsibilities of people in authority in school and community settings.
u1749 [T1 R13 C3] B. (2-3) Describe the basic structure of government at the local, state, and Tribal levels, including the importance of citizen participation.
u1750 [T1 R13 C4] B. (4-5) Explain the structure, responsibilities, and powers exercised by officials of the national government and describe ways informed citizens can and should participate.
u1751 [T1 R13 C6] B. (6-8) Compare the powers and responsibilities of the United States government to other forms of government, including the roles and rights of citizens.
u1752 [T1 R13 C8] B. (9-12) Evaluate the impact of the structure and powers exercised by governmental systems on public policy, using historical and contemporary examples.
u1753 [T1 R14 C1] C. (K-1) Describe the purposes of rules in various settings (e.g., family, classroom, school), how rules are made, and consequences for violating rules.
u1754 [T1 R14 C3] C. (2-3) Explain the purposes of laws and government in the community and state, examining the role of elected officials in making laws.
u1755 [T1 R14 C4] C. (4-5) Examine the principles of the Constitution of the United States, including how laws are made in a democratic society and are used to protect individual rights.
u1756 [T1 R14 C6] C. (6-8) Examine the impact of constitutions, laws, treaties, and international agreements on the limits of government, including the concepts of sovereignty and the rule of law.
u1757 [T1 R14 C8] C. (9-12) Analyze the impact of constitutions, laws, treaties, and international agreements, by comparing how various governmental powers and responsibilities have changed over time.
u1758 [T1 R16 C2] 2. The student will develop skills which demonstrate an understanding of historical events and the people who shaped our history.

u1761 [T1 R1 C1] A. (K-1) Identify a primary source of information and with support, gather basic information (i.e., author, date, facts).
u1762 [T1 R1 C3] A. (2-3) Explain the difference between a primary and secondary source of information; gather basic information (i.e., author, date, facts).
u1763 [T1 R1 C4] A. (4-5) Compare and analyze primary and secondary sources from the past and present, including the intended audience and author’s purpose.
u1764 [T1 R1 C5] A. (6-8) Gather and draw conclusions from sources of evidence, identifying plausible author, occasion, audience, purpose, and possible bias.
u1765 [T1 R1 C6] A. (9-12) Gather and evaluate the usefulness of various formats of evidence for specific inquiry, analyzing the broader historical context, and assessing potential bias and credibility of sources.
u1766 [T1 R2 C1] B. (K-1) Identify point of view and examples relevant to the student’s experiences.
u1767 [T1 R2 C3] B. (2-3) Define point of view and give examples relevant to the student’s experiences.
u1768 [T1 R2 C4] B. (4-5) Compare perspectives of individuals and groups from different regions and experiences, including the past and present.
u1769 [T1 R2 C5] B. (6-8) Describe multiple factors and explain how they can influence the perspectives of individuals and groups on events from history and the modern era.
u1770 [T1 R2 C6] B. (9-12) Analyze complex and interacting factors that influence multiple perspectives during different historical eras and contemporary events.
u1771 [T1 R3 C1] C. (K-1) Discuss possible reasons for an event and make simple timelines which reflect cause-effect relationships, with guidance and support.
u1772 [T1 R3 C3] C. (2-3) Generate possible reasons for an event and draw conclusions from simple timelines by identifying immediate cause and effect relationships.
u1773 [T1 R3 C4] C. (4-5) Explain multiple causes and effects of events and developments of the past or present; create timelines to identify multiple causes and effects from given information.
u1774 [T1 R3 C5] C. (6-8) Distinguish multiple causation, including immediate versus long-term cause-effect relationships; construct timelines of related events.
u1775 [T1 R3 C6] C. (9-12) Evaluate how multiple, complex events are shaped by unique circumstances of time and place; construct and interpret parallel timelines.

u1781 [T1 R8 C5] A. (6-8) Answer geographic questions and conduct investigations by acquiring, organizing, and interpreting information about the modern world and historical events.
u1782 [T1 R8 C6] A. (9-12) Actively engage in asking and answering geographic questions by acquiring, organizing, and analyzing multiple sources of data and information about the world’s past and present.

u1819 [T2 R2 C1] III. Critical Reading and Interpretation of Informational Sources: Students will engage in critical, active reading of primary and secondary sources related to social studies concepts.
u1820 [T2 R5 C2] K-Grade 1
u1821 [T2 R5 C5] Grades 2-3
u1822 [T2 R5 C8] Grades 4-5
u1823 [T2 R5 C11] Grades 6-8

u1825 [T2 R8 C2] 1. The student will comprehend, evaluate, and synthesize textual sources to acquire and refine knowledge in the social studies.
u1826 [T2 R10 C1] A. (PK-1) Locate the topic or main idea and supporting details of a text.
u1827 [T2 R10 C4] A. (2-3) Summarize and the main idea and locate supporting details of a text (e.g., primary and secondary sources).
u1828 [T2 R10 C7] A. (3-4) Use information accurately from a text when explaining the text explicitly and when drawing inferences from the text.
u1829 [T2 R10 C10] A. (6-8) Paraphrase the main idea and cite evidence from primary and secondary sources; provide an accurate summary of a source distinct from prior knowledge or opinion.
u1830 [T2 R10 C13] A. (9-12) Cite specific textual evidence to support analysis of primary and secondary sources, evaluating features such as author, date, and origin of information.
u1831 [T2 R11 C1] B. (PK-1) Use titles and graphic features, including photographs and illustrations, to understand a text.
u1832 [T2 R11 C4] B. (2-3) Use graphic features of a text (e.g., photographs, titles, headings, subheadings, charts, graphs, illustrations) to understand content.
u1833 [T2 R11 C7] B. (4-5) Use information from multiple print or digital sources (e.g., timelines, maps, graphs, political cartoons, images, artwork) to answer a question.
u1834 [T2 R11 C10] B. (6-8) Integrate the use of visual information (e.g., maps, charts, photographs, videos, political cartoons, artwork) with textual information from primary and secondary sources to draw conclusions.
u1835 [T2 R11 C13] B. (9-12) Analyze information from visual, oral, digital, and interactive texts (e.g., maps, charts, images, political cartoons, videos, artwork) in order to draw conclusions and defend arguments.

u1881 [T1 R4 C2] 2. The student will engage in authentic research to acquire, refine, and share knowledge through written presentations and products.
u1882 [T1 R6 C1] A. (PK-1) With guidance and support, generate a list of topics of interest and questions about social studies.
